17

18

20

21

22

17

18

19

20

21 
future net salvage.  As a result, the Depreciation Studies result in fairly significant increases 
22

in depreciation expense even with no specific adjustments related to the impacts of the 
1

2

3

4

Q. 
Given these considerations, how would you characterize the results of the 
6 
Depreciation Studies? 
7 
A. 
The Companies’ proposals, based on the Depreciation Studies, are more similar to 
8

9 
salvage are, while significant, still below the negative net salvage indications from the 
10 
historical data, meaning that the data could support an even larger increase in depreciation.  
11 
Thus, while the studies result in increases in depreciation expense, due to the considerations 
12 
discussed above, I believe that further increases in depreciation will be needed in the future.  
13 
Not only are the Companies’ proposals in this case best considered a first step in moving 
14 
toward more appropriate depreciation rates, but they are, in my judgment, at the lower end 
15 
of the range of reasonableness for the Companies’ depreciation rates.  Stated differently, 
16 
my recommendations already temper needed increases in depreciation by (1) incorporating 
17 
net salvage estimates that are less negative than those supported by the Companies’ 
18

19

20

21

II. 
DEPRECIATION STUDIES
1 
Q.
Please define the concept of depreciation. 
2 
A.
The FERC Uniform System of Accounts for gas utilities defines depreciation as follows: 
3 
Depreciation, as applied to depreciable gas plant, means the loss in service 
4 
value not restored by current maintenance, incurred in connection with the 
5 
consumption or prospective retirement of gas plant in the course of service 
6 
from causes which are known to be in current operation and against which 
7 
the utility is not protected by insurance. Among the causes to be given 
8 
consideration are wear and tear, decay, action of the elements, inadequacy, 
9 
obsolescence, changes in the art, changes in demand and requirements of 
10 
public authorities, and, in the case of natural gas companies, the exhaustion 
11 
of natural resources.2 
12 
Note that the Uniform System of Accounts specifically includes obsolescence, changes in 
13 
demand, and requirements of public authorities as factors that should be considered for 
14 
depreciation.   
15 
16 
Q.
How does depreciation impact a utility and customers from a financial standpoint? 
17 
A.
Depreciation is both an important and complicated concept because it affects utility 
18 
accounting, and, as a result, both customer rates and earnings, in more than one way.  From 
19 
a ratemaking standpoint, depreciation is often best understood as an expense incorporated 
20 
into a utility’s revenue requirement.  However, depreciation is also an expense on a 
21 
company’s books and, as an expense, annual depreciation accruals are a reduction to a 
22 
company’s earnings.  Depreciation, therefore, has an effect on both revenues and expenses. 
23 
This is consistent with the concept of the “matching principle” used in accounting, which 
24 
2 18 C.F.R. §201 (Gas FERC Uniform System of Accounts), Definition 12.  


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 10 of 48 
 
requires the recognition of expenses on the income statement in the same period in which 
1 
the related revenues are earned.  Depreciation Systems, a widely cited depreciation text, 
2 
explains the matching principle as: 
3 
The matching principle requires that all revenues earned during a given time 
4 
period be matched with all the expenses incurred in that same time period 
5 
to produce these revenues.3  
6 
It follows from this principle that the depreciation of capital assets that have lives that 
7 
extend beyond one year should be recognized over the useful lives of the assets.  As noted 
8 
by the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners (“NARUC”), a cost 
9 
allocation concept for depreciation “satisfies the accounting principle of matching expense 
10 
and revenues.”    
11 
 
12 
From a ratemaking standpoint, depreciation impacts both revenues from customers, as 
13 
annual depreciation expense is part of the revenue requirement, and the expenses charged 
14 
to a given year.  If depreciation is such that the depreciation expense over the life of an 
15 
asset recovers the full cost of the asset (which includes the cost to retire the asset at the end 
16 
of its life), then customer rates established based on these depreciation accruals will 
17 
provide a return of capital to utility investors who originally provided the capital to invest 
18 
in the asset.   
19 
 
20 
 
3 Wolf and Fitch, Depreciation Systems (1994), p. 5. 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 11 of 48 
 
A related concept in utility ratemaking is “intergenerational equity,” in which customers 
1 
who receive the benefit of a utility asset should pay their fair share of the costs of the asset.  
2 
If too much depreciation is reflected in the early years of an asset’s life, then customers in 
3 
the early years will pay a disproportionate share of the cost of the asset, while customers 
4 
in later years will pay less than their fair share.  Conversely, if depreciation is too low in 
5 
the earlier years, then the costs of the asset would be unfairly borne by a future generation 
6 
of customers. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Does the depreciation of assets that has been recorded in prior years also affect 
9 
customer rates? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  There is also an additional way depreciation affects both customer rates and a 
11 
company’s earnings.  Accumulated depreciation, which is effectively a running total of 
12 
depreciation that has occurred in the past, is a reduction to rate base.  As a result, while 
13 
higher depreciation will have a short-term impact of increasing the revenue requirement in 
14 
a given year, its longer term impact will be to reduce rate base and, in turn, the return on 
15 
rate base.  The converse is also true.  Lower depreciation expense will have a short-term 
16 
effect of reducing the revenue requirement but over the long term will result in a higher 
17 
rate base and a higher return on rate base.  Thus, when considering the impact on customers 
18 
due to depreciation, there are both short term and long term impacts to consider and often 
19 
these impacts move in the opposite direction from one another. 
20 
 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 12 of 48 
 
This concept is explained by NARUC: 
1 
The regulatory body prescribing depreciation rates is thus confronted with 
2 
a decision which affects both short run and long run interests of the 
3 
customer and the company.  If a commission prescribes rates which yield 
4 
depreciation accruals that are too low, the revenue requirement in the short 
5 
run may be lower.  But the requirements for income taxes and return may 
6 
offset the apparent savings in depreciation expense, so service rates in the 
7 
long run may be higher.  If depreciation rates are set so low that the revenue 
8 
requirement fails to repay the capital invested in a group of property by the 
9 
end of its service life, confiscation takes place or the unpaid cost remains in 
10 
the rate base until amortized or expensed.  On the other hand, if the 
11 
regulatory body establishes depreciation rates toward the upper end of the 
12 
zone of reasonableness, rates for service will be higher in the short run, but 
13 
may be lower in the long run.4 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
In preparing the Depreciation Studies, did you follow generally accepted practices in 
16 
the field of depreciation? 
17 
A. 
Yes. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Are the methods and procedures used in the Depreciation Studies consistent with the 
20 
Companies’ past practices? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  I used the same methods and procedures to calculate depreciation as those used in 
22 
past depreciation studies conducted for the Companies, as well as depreciation studies 
23 
presented by other companies in rate proceedings before the Commission.  The approach 
24 
is to determine depreciation rates based on the straight-line method, the broad group 
25 
 
4 See, NARUC, Public Utility Depreciation Practices (1996), p. 23 (emphasis added).

Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 13 of 48 
 
average service life procedure, and the whole life technique.  I used Iowa-type survivor 
1 
curves to estimate service lives. 
2 
 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the presentation of the Depreciation Studies in your exhibits. 
4 
A. 
The Depreciation Studies, set forth in Exhibit___ (NWA-1) and Exhibit___(NWA-2), are 
5 
presented in nine parts.  Part I, Introduction, presents the scope and basis for the 
6 
Depreciation Studies.  Parts II through V include descriptions of the methods and 
7 
procedures used for the estimation of survivor curves and net salvage and the calculation 
8 
of annual depreciation and the theoretical reserve.  Part VI, Results of Study, presents a 
9 
description of the results and a summary of the depreciation calculations.  Parts VII through 
10 
IX present graphs and tables that relate to the service life analyses, the net salvage analyses, 
11 
and the detailed depreciation calculations. 
12 
 
13 
The tables on pages VI-5 through VI-10 of Exhibit___ (NWA-1) and pages VI-5 through 
14 
VI-9 of Exhibit___(NWA-2), present the estimated survivor curves, the net salvage 
15 
percentages, the original cost of plant, and the book depreciation reserve as of December 
16 
31, 2021, and the calculated annual depreciation accrual and applicable depreciation rate 
17 
for each plant account or subaccount.  The section beginning on page VII-1 presents the 
18 
results of the retirement rate analyses prepared as the historical bases for the service life 
19 
estimates.  The section beginning on page VIII-1 presents the results of the net salvage 
20 
analysis.  The section beginning on page IX-1 presents the depreciation calculations related 
21 
to surviving original cost as of December 31, 2021.   
22

Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 14 of 48 
 
Q. 
Please explain how the results of the Depreciation Studies were determined. 
1 
A. 
The Depreciation Studies included as Exhibit ___(NWA-1) and Exhibit___(NWA-2) used 
2 
the straight line, whole life method of depreciation, with the broad group average service 
3 
life procedure.  The annual depreciation is based on a method of depreciation accounting 
4 
that seeks to distribute the service value (original cost of plant assets plus estimated costs 
5 
of removal less estimated net salvage at the time of retirement) over the estimated service 
6 
life of each group of assets in a systematic and rational manner. 
7 
 
 
8 
 
In addition to the depreciation rates in the Depreciation Studies using the straight-line 
9 
method, I have also provided depreciation rates using the units-of-production method based 
10 
on the Companies’ Medium Electrification – CEV gas throughput and customer count 
11 
estimates.  These have been provided in Exhibit  ___(NWA-5) and Exhibit ___(NWA-6).  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How did you determine the recommended annual depreciation accrual rates? 
14 
A. 
This was done in two phases.  In the first phase, I developed estimates of the service life 
15 
and net salvage factors for each depreciable group (that is, each plant account or subaccount 
16 
identified as having similar characteristics).  In the second phase, I calculated the annual 
17 
depreciation accrual rates using the applicable average service lives and net salvage factors. 
18 
 
19 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 15 of 48 
 
Q. 
What part does the average service life play in the determination of depreciation 
1 
rates?  
2 
A. 
The average service life is the period over which the original cost of plant will be 
3 
depreciated.  As a simplified example, for an average service life of 25 years, the annual 
4 
depreciation is 1/25, or four percent, of the original cost of the plant before taking into 
5 
account the net salvage factor.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
What is the effect on annual depreciation expense of a change to an average service 
8 
life? 
9 
A. 
The depreciation expense accrual varies inversely with its underlying average service life, 
10 
and all else being equal, the longer the average service life, the lower the annual 
11 
depreciation rate and the lower the annual depreciation expense.  Conversely, the shorter 
12 
the average service life, the higher the annual depreciation rate and the higher the annual 
13 
depreciation expense.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What part does net salvage play in the determination of depreciation rates? 
16 
A. 
Depreciation is intended to recover the full costs of the Companies’ assets over the period 
17 
of time they are in service.  The full cost of an asset includes both the original cost when 
18 
the asset was installed and the net salvage at the end of the asset’s life.  Thus, in addition 
19 
to providing for recovery of the original cost of plant over its estimated average service 
20 
life, annual depreciation rates include an estimated net salvage factor.  The purpose of this 
21 
estimated net salvage factor is to reflect, over the life of the plant, the expected gross 
22 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 16 of 48 
 
salvage value of plant less the expected cost of removal upon retirement.  With few 
1 
exceptions, most plant assets result in negative net salvage upon retirement, which means 
2 
that removal costs exceed gross salvage value.  Gross salvage and removal cost values are 
3 
netted, expressed as a percentage of the plant’s original cost, and included in the annual 
4 
depreciation rate.  As a result, and in accordance with basic depreciation principles and the 
5 
Uniform System of Accounts, the service value of an asset is allocated evenly over the 
6 
estimated useful life of the asset. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the first phase of the Depreciation Studies in which you estimated the 
9 
average service life and net salvage factors for each plant account or subaccount. 
10 
A. 
The service life and net salvage study consisted of compiling historical data from records 
11 
related to the Companies’ plant; analyzing the data to obtain historical trends of survivor 
12 
characteristics; obtaining supplementary information from management and operating 
13 
personnel concerning practices and plans as they relate to plant operations; making visits 
14 
to various sites to view the physical condition of facilities; and interpreting the data and 
15 
information along with the average service lives and net salvage factors used by other 
16 
electric and gas utilities to form judgments of average service lives and net salvage factors 
17 
applicable to the Companies’ plant and equipment. 
18 
 
19 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 17 of 48 
 
Q. 
You mentioned that preparing the Depreciation Studies involved visits to the 
1 
Companies’ facilities.  What is the significance of the visits? 
2 
A. 
I conducted a field review of KEDNY and KEDLI property as part of the Depreciation 
3 
Studies during October 2022.  Depreciation studies should not be limited only to statistical 
4 
analysis or visual comparisons of smoothed survivor curves based on actual mortality 
5 
experience and standardized survivor curves.  Field reviews, as well as discussions with 
6 
operating and engineering personnel, are conducted to become familiar with company 
7 
operations and obtain an understanding of the function of the plant and information with 
8 
respect to the reasons for past retirements and the expected causes of future retirements.  I 
9 
incorporated this knowledge, as well as information from other discussions with 
10 
management, in the interpretation and extrapolation of the statistical analyses. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What historical data did you analyze to estimate average service lives? 
13 
A. 
I analyzed the Companies’ accounting entries that record plant asset transactions for the 
14 
period 2001 through 2021 for most accounts.  These transactions included additions, 
15 
retirements, transfers, and related balances.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
What method did you use to analyze the data? 
18 
A. 
I used the retirement rate method.  This is the most appropriate method when retirement 
19 
data, covering a long period of time, is available because it determines the average rates of 
20 
retirement experienced by the Companies during the period of time covered by the 
21 
Depreciation Studies.  It is also the method used in past depreciation studies and is the 
22 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 18 of 48 
 
overwhelmingly predominant approach used in depreciation studies across the country 
1 
when aged data are available. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe how you used the retirement rate method to analyze the Companies' 
4 
service life data. 
5 
A. 
I performed the retirement rate analysis for each different group of property, generally a 
6 
particular plant account, in the Depreciation Studies.  For each property group, I used the 
7 
retirement rate data to form life tables which, when plotted, show original survivor curves 
8 
for that property group.  Each original survivor curve represents the average survivor 
9 
pattern experienced by the vintage groups during the experience and placement band 
10 
studied.  Because original survivor curves do not include the complete experience of most 
11 
vintages of plant and because future survivor characteristics may be different from those 
12 
experienced in the past, the experienced survivor patterns do not necessarily describe the 
13 
property group’s life characteristics.  Therefore, interpretation of the original survivor 
14 
curves is required for them to be valid considerations in estimating future service lives.  
15 
Standard survivor curves, such as the Iowa-type survivor curves, are used to perform these 
16 
interpretations.   
17 
 
18 
Q. 
What is an “Iowa-type survivor curve” and how can it be used to estimate the average 
19 
service life characteristics for each property group? 
20 
A. 
Iowa-type survivor curves are a widely-used group of survivor curves that contain the range 
21 
of survivor characteristics usually experienced by utilities and other industrial companies.  
22 


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 19 of 48 
 
The Iowa curves were developed at the Iowa State College Engineering Experiment Station 
1 
through an extensive process of observing and classifying the ages at which various types 
2 
of property used by utilities and other industrial companies had been retired.   
3 
 
4 
Iowa type curves are used to smooth and extrapolate original survivor curves determined 
5 
by the retirement rate method.   The Iowa curves can be used to describe the forecast rates 
6 
of retirement based on the observed rates of retirement and the outlook for future 
7 
retirements. 
8 
 
9 
The estimated survivor curve designations for each depreciable property group indicate the 
10 
average service life, the family within the Iowa system to which the property group 
11 
belongs, and the relative height of the mode.  Take the Iowa 50-R1.5, for example.  The 
12 
first designation indicates an average service life of 50 years.  The second designation 
13 
indicates a right-moded, or R, type curve (the mode occurs after average life for right-
14 
moded curves).  The third designation indicates a relatively low height of 1.5, for the mode 
15 
(possible modes for R-type curves range from 1 to 5).  The Iowa curves are discussed in 
16 
more detail in Part II of Exhibit___(NWA-1) and Exhibit___(NWA-2). 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
What is the “h-system” of survivor curves? 
19 
A. 
The h-system of survivor curves was developed in 1947 by Bradford Kimball of the 
20 
Commission.  Similar to the Iowa curves, the h-curves are labeled in accordance with the 
21 
relative height of the modes of the associated retirement frequency curves.  Thus, for 
22 


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 20 of 48 
 
example a 50-h3.0 indicates a 50-year average service life and a mid-mode curve (modes 
1 
for the h-system curves range from 0.0 to 5.0). 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What type of survivor curves did you use for the Depreciation Studies? 
4 
A. 
For the Depreciation Studies, we used Iowa-type survivor curves.  This represents a change 
5 
from the h-type curves used in the Companies’ previous depreciation studies.  However, 
6 
the Iowa curves are, to our knowledge, used in every U.S. jurisdiction, including in New 
7 
York by most of the electric and gas utilities in the State.  In contrast, the h-curves, to my 
8 
knowledge, are not used anywhere outside of New York and have not been consistently 
9 
used by utilities in the State.  Further, the h-curves tend to have long “tails,” meaning that 
10 
these curves forecast that a portion of property will survive much longer than the average 
11 
service life of a given depreciable group.  These types of life characteristics are not 
12 
common for most types of utility property.  In part for this reason, the Iowa curves typically 
13 
provide a more reasonable retirement dispersion pattern for most types of utility assets.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What approach did you use to estimate the lives of significant facilities such as gas 
16 
storage facilities? 
17 
A. 
I used the life span method to estimate the lives of significant facilities for which concurrent 
18 
retirement of the entire facility is anticipated, which is applicable to the gas Liquified 
19 
Natural Gas (“LNG”) plants.  In this method, the survivor characteristics of such facilities 
20 
are described using interim survivor curves and estimated probable retirement dates.   
21 
 
22 


--- Page 23 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 21 of 48 
 
The interim survivor curves describe the rate of retirement related to replacements elements 
1 
of the facility, such as piping, vaporizers, compressors and purification equipment.  The 
2 
probable retirement date provides the final retirement rate for each year of installation for 
3 
the facility by truncating the interim survivor curve for each installation year at its attained 
4 
age at the date of probable retirement.  The use of interim survivor curves truncated at the 
5 
date of probable retirement provides a consistent method for estimating the lives of assets 
6 
installed over multiple years for a particular facility, inasmuch as a single concurrent 
7 
retirement for all years of installation will occur when it is retired. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Have the Companies previously used the life span method? 
10 
A. 
Not for these accounts.  However, the life-span method is the preferred method for these 
11 
types of assets because it provides a consistent way to depreciate the various vintages of 
12 
plant installed at facilities such as LNG plants.  The life span method has been accepted by 
13 
many public utility commissions across the United States and Canada, including the 
14 
Commission.  It is currently used, for example, for Con Edison’s liquefied natural gas 
15 
(“LNG”) plant assets. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
What are the bases for the probable retirement dates that you have estimated for each 
18 
facility? 
19 
A. 
The probable retirement dates are based on estimated life spans that reflect consideration 
20 
of the age, use, size, nature of construction, management outlook and typical life spans 
21 
experienced and used by other utilities for similar facilities.  For certain facilities, the life 
22 


--- Page 24 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 22 of 48 
 
spans result in probable retirement years that are many years in the future.  The retirements 
1 
of these facilities are not yet subject to specific management plans, as such plans would be 
2 
premature.  At the appropriate time, detailed studies of the economics of rehabilitation and 
3 
continued use or retirement of the facility may be performed, and the results incorporated 
4 
in the estimation of the facility’s life span.  However, to allocate the costs of these facilities 
5 
properly, a probable retirement date must be estimated based on the information available 
6 
today.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Is the life span method consistent with the whole life technique? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  The life span method is a method of determining the average service life and 
10 
dispersion pattern for each vintage of plant within a depreciable group.  This method can, 
11 
therefore, be used with either the whole life or the remaining life technique.  When using 
12 
the life span method with the whole life technique, as is used in the Depreciation Studies, 
13 
the average service life is calculated for each vintage based on the estimated retirement 
14 
date and interim survivor curve.  The average service life is then used to calculate 
15 
depreciation expense. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please provide an example of how the annual depreciation accrual rate for a 
18 
particular plant account is presented in the Depreciation Studies. 
19 
A. 
I will use KEDLI plant Account 376.08, Mains – Steel and Account 376.18, Mains - 
20 
Plastic, which were studied together and combined comprise one of the larger depreciable 
21 
accounts, as an example.  I used the retirement rate method to analyze the survivor

characteristics of this property group.  Aged plant accounting data was compiled from 2001 
through 2021 and analyzed in periods that best represent the overall service life of this

3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please describe how you determined the proposed net salvage factors. 
5 
A. 
Consistent with well-established industry practices, I determined the net salvage factors by 
6 
considering relevant information such as the results of historical net salvage analyses, the 
7 
existing net salvage rates in effect, the Companies’ current practices with regard to net 
8 
salvage, and the net salvage factors used by other gas companies.  
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the statistical net salvage analyses. 
11 
A. 
In the statistical net salvage analyses, net salvage is expressed as a percentage of the book 
12 
cost of plant retired by calendar year.  This method provides a statistical basis for the level 
13 
of net salvage that can be expected to occur in the future.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Are the net salvage analyses and approach you used to reflect net salvage in 
16 
depreciation rates consistent with authoritative depreciation texts? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  NARUC’s Public Utility Depreciation Practices and Wolf and Fitch’s Depreciation 
18 
Systems (“Wolf and Fitch”) are well-regarded texts that are considered to be authoritative 
19 
depreciation sources by depreciation professionals.  These texts describe the method of 
20 
estimating net salvage and explain that expected net salvage at the time of retirement of 
21 


--- Page 27 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 25 of 48 
 
plant assets is expressed as a percentage of original cost of the plant that will be retired and 
1 
is estimated using the same methods I have employed.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Are the methods used in the Depreciation Studies for the net salvage analysis widely 
4 
accepted in the industry? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  The net salvage analysis used in the Depreciation Studies is the predominant approach 
6 
in the utility industry.  In the vast majority of jurisdictions, including New York, a portion 
7 
of depreciation expense includes a provision for the prospective recovery of future net 
8 
salvage over the service life of the underlying assets, and the net salvage factors are 
9 
estimated using the same methods used in the Depreciation Studies.  This approach is 
10 
consistent with the Uniform System of Accounts, the ratemaking practices of most other 
11 
state regulatory commissions, and the ratemaking approach of the FERC. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What have you proposed for gas assets expected to be retired as a result of the 
14 
Companies’ leak-prone pipe replacement program? 
15 
A. 
For the Depreciation Studies, the Companies’ mains and services accounts were segregated 
16 
into the portion that will be retired through the Companies’ pipe replacement programs and 
17 
all other mains and services.  Both KEDNY and KEDLI plan to complete their respective 
18 
programs by 2045.  Accordingly, the proposal for the assets that are planned to be replaced, 
19 
which consist primarily of older cast iron and bare steel pipe, is to recover the remaining 
20 
costs by 2045.  This is similar to the approach currently used for KEDLI as approved in 
21 
Case 19-G-0309, for the Companies’ affiliate Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation in Case 
22

20-G-0381, as well as recently adopted for O&R and Con Edison (a settlement for the 
1 
latter, which includes the recovery of leak-prone pipe assets, is currently pending before 
2 
the Commission in Case 22-G-0065). 
3 
 
4 
III. 
TEST OF THE BOOK RESERVES 
5 
Q. 
What is the book reserve? 
6 
A. 
The book reserve, also referred to as the “book accumulated depreciation” or the 
7 
“accumulated provision for depreciation,” is a running total of historical depreciation 
8 
activity.  It is equal to the historical depreciation accruals, less retirements and cost of 
9 
removal, plus historical gross salvage.  The book reserve also represents a reduction to the 
10 
original cost of plant when calculating rate base. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What is the theoretical reserve? 
13 
A. 
The theoretical reserve is an estimate of the accumulated depreciation based on the current 
14 
plant balances and depreciation parameters (service life and net salvage estimates) at a 
15 
specific point in time.  It is equal to the portion of the depreciable cost of plant that will not 
16 
be allocated to expense through future whole-life depreciation accruals based on the current 
17 
forecasts of service life and net salvage.  The theoretical reserve is also referred to as the 
18 
Calculated Accrued Depreciation (“CAD”). 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 29 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 27 of 48 
 
Q. 
What is a theoretical reserve imbalance?  
1 
A. 
A theoretical reserve imbalance is calculated as the difference between a company’s book 
2 
accumulated depreciation, or book reserve, and the calculated accrued depreciation, or 
3 
theoretical reserve.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
How did you analyze the reserve positions for the Companies? 
6 
A. 
To test the adequacy of the book reserve for depreciation, I compared the book reserve at 
7 
year-end to the theoretical reserve calculated using average service lives, survivor curves, 
8 
and net salvage factors based on the Depreciation Studies.  The results are summarized in 
9 
Table 2 of Exhibit __(NWA-1) and Exhibit___(NWA-2).  The difference between the book 
10 
and theoretical reserves can be expressed both in total dollars and as a percentage of the 
11 
theoretical reserve.  Results of such a study can indicate either a positive theoretical reserve 
12 
imbalance (sometimes referred to as a “book reserve excess”) or a negative theoretical 
13 
reserve imbalance (sometimes referred to as a “book reserve deficiency”).  For example, a 
14 
book reserve of $190 and a theoretical reserve of $200 would result in a book reserve 
15 
deficiency of $10, or five percent. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Is it common to have a non-zero theoretical reserve imbalance? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  Service life and net salvage estimates can change over time, and these estimates are 
19 
updated when a new study is performed.  It is expected that there will be some variation 
20 
between the book and theoretical reserves.  However, because New York has historically 
21 
used whole-life rates, rather than remaining life depreciation rates (which automatically 
22 


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 28 of 48 
 
correct for any reserve variations), corrective action is often required.  At a minimum, 
1 
corrective action should be taken when the theoretical reserve imbalance is large.  In New 
2 
York, corrective action has traditionally been taken when the variation exceeds 10 percent 
3 
of the theoretical reserve.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please summarize the theoretical reserve imbalances.   
6 
A. 
As shown in Table 2 of Exhibit___(NWA-1), the book reserve for KEDNY as of December 
7 
31, 2021, is $1,487,099,091.  The theoretical reserve resulting from the Depreciation 
8 
Studies is $1,885,067,159, resulting in a reserve deficiency of $397,968,068, or 27 percent 
9 
of the book reserve.  Similarly, the book reserve for KEDLI, shown in Table 2 of 
10 
Exhibit___(NWA-2) as of December 31, 2021, is $819,721,873, the theoretical reserve 
11 
resulting from the Depreciation Studies is $1,414,736,378, which results in a reserve 
12 
deficiency of $595,014,505, or 73 percent of the book reserve.  These amounts exclude the 
13 
balances for leak-prone pipe assets, which have a separate recovery and, therefore, are not 
14 
included in the amortization of any reserve deficiencies. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ proposal regarding the reserve deficiencies for each 
17 
company? 
18 
A. 
The Companies’ proposal is consistent with the traditional approach that has been used in 
19 
New York.  The Companies propose to amortize the portions of the reserve deficiencies 
20 
that exceed 10 percent of the book reserve over a 20-year period.  For KEDNY, this results 
21 


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 29 of 48 
 
in an annual amortization of $12,462,908 and for KEDLI it results in an amortization of 
1 
$25,652,116 per year. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Do you believe the traditional approach in New York is the optimal approach to 
4 
address reserve deficiencies? 
5 
A. 
No.  The approach of amortizing only the portion of a reserve deficiency will not recover 
6 
the full costs of a company’s assets over their service lives (since there is no recovery of 
7 
the portion less than 10 percent of the book or theoretical reserve).  Further, to the extent 
8 
there are inconsistencies in the implementation of reserve deficiency amortizations (for 
9 
example, in some instances 10 percent of the book reserve, such as in prior studies for the 
10 
Companies, but in others 10 percent of the theoretical reserve), the approach of using the 
11 
whole-life technique with reserve amortizations is less systematic and rational. 
12 
 
 
13 
 
New York is one of only three states that do not use the remaining life technique which, as 
14 
its widespread adoption suggests, is generally considered to be a better approach because 
15 
any reserve deficiencies are automatically addressed over the remaining lives of a 
16 
company’s assets.  Particularly in the context of the CLCPA, I believe the Commission 
17 
should reconsider use of the remaining life technique.  However, for the purposes of this 
18 
study and in the context of a more gradual movement to the most reasonable levels of 
19 
depreciation, the Companies’ proposal is consistent with past practices in New York. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 32 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 30 of 48 
 
IV. 
THE CLCPA AND THE GAS PLANNING PROCEEDING 
1 
Q. 
Do the requirements set forth in the CLCPA and the Final Scoping Plan impact 
2 
depreciation for gas assets? 
3 
A. 
Yes.   I believe there is a broad expectation that the CLCPA will mean that the future of 
4 
the gas industry will be different from the past.  For example, the CAC’s Final Scoping 
5 
Plan finds that “[a]ll the information before the Climate Action Council (Council) indicates 
6 
that achievement of the emissions limits will entail a substantial reduction of fossil natural 
7 
gas use and strategic downsizing and decarbonization of the gas system” (p. 350).  The 
8 
Final Scoping Plan further states, “[a]ll scenarios modeled in the integration analysis 
9 
include rapid adoption of high-efficiency heat pumps so that one to two million energy-
10 
efficient homes use heat pumps by 2030, and by 2050, the large majority of buildings 
11 
statewide use electric heat pumps for heating, cooling and hot water” (p. 176). 
12 
 
 
13 
 
If the future transpires as envisioned in the scoping plan, there will be profound 
14 
implications on the most equitable and reasonable recovery of capital investments through 
15 
depreciation.  A significant portion of the Companies’ current plant investments are for 
16 
assets that have historically had long lives – often 60 years or more.  If the “large majority 
17 
of buildings” will displace the use of gas with electric heat pumps over the next 30 years, 
18 
then it is highly likely that the Companies’ current asset base will have shorter lives than 
19 
has been the case historically.  This is particularly true if policymakers and regulators 
20 
pursue large-scale zonal electrification.   
21 
 
 
22 


--- Page 33 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 31 of 48 
 
 
Further, a strategic downsizing of the gas system would mean fewer customers and lower 
1 
sales volumes than in the past.  As a result, not only would lives be shorter but it may be 
2 
more consistent with the principle of intergenerational equity to allocate a higher 
3 
proportion of capital costs through depreciation to today’s larger customer base so as to 
4 
avoid disproportionally harming the smaller future customer base that will not be able to 
5 
fully electrify. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How will the emissions reductions in the CLCPA impact depreciation expense? 
8 
A. 
There are three main aspects of depreciation that could be impacted by significant changes 
9 
in gas consumption.  The first is the useful lives of the Companies’ assets.  Assets may 
10 
have shorter service lives than has been the case historically.  For example, if a customer 
11 
decides to fully electrify their energy usage, the infrastructure providing gas service to that 
12 
customer would be retired.  With widespread electrification, this would result in shorter 
13 
service lives for assets such as gas services, meters, and meter installations.  Gas mains and 
14 
regulator stations would also be affected if gas throughput declines, as many of these 
15 
facilities could become obsolete.  Other assets may also become obsolete if they are no 
16 
longer needed due to declines in gas throughput. 
17 
 
18 
The second aspect that could be affected is cost of removal.  Under normal utility 
19 
operations, cost of removal often occurs for replacement projects.  When gas mains and 
20 
services are replaced, pipe is typically retired in place (although there are costs to cut, cap 
21 
and purge any gas from the pipe).  However, it is possible these costs could be different in 
22 


--- Page 34 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 32 of 48 
 
the future if, for example, portions of the gas system are electrified as a whole and specific 
1 
assets are required to be removed, rather than retired in place.  It may also be more costly 
2 
to retire and decommission obsolete mains and services than the retirement costs associated 
3 
with replacement projects in which various costs (such as equipment and paving) may be 
4 
shared between the addition of the new asset and cost of removal.   
5 
 
6 
Lastly, the depreciation method used to allocate capital costs may need to be reconsidered.  
7 
Traditionally, almost all utilities have used the straight-line method of depreciation in 
8 
which capital costs are allocated equally over the service lives of the assets – depreciation 
9 
is calculated so that equal amounts are recorded in each year of an asset’s estimated service 
10 
life.  Straight-line depreciation works well when utilities have fairly stable or increasing 
11 
demand, as the annual depreciation accruals tend to approximate the consumption on the 
12 
system.  If, however, consumption were to decline significantly, a question arises whether 
13 
it is equitable to have equal depreciation charges today as in the future when there will be 
14 
less consumption.  An alternative method to consider is the units-of-production method, 
15 
which is discussed in more detail later in this testimony. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Earlier in your testimony, you indicated that you performed the depreciation studies 
18 
for the Companies in the Gas Planning Proceeding.  Please discuss these studies 
19 
further. 
20 
A. 
Yes.  I prepared the Companies’ Gas Planning Depreciation Studies and my firm, Gannett 
21 
Fleming, prepared similar such studies for each of the other Joint Utilities.  These studies 
22 


--- Page 35 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 33 of 48 
 
assessed the impact of different “business assumptions” (i.e., assumptions about future gas 
1 
throughput and the number of customers on the system) as well as the revenue and bill 
2 
impacts for different “depreciation approaches” (i.e., methods of depreciation) for each set 
3 
of business assumptions.  Business assumptions considered included a business-as-usual 
4 
case, as well as high and medium electrification scenarios.  Depreciation approaches 
5 
analyzed include the current straight-line method, recovering some or all assets by 2050, 
6 
and the units-of-production method based on the throughput declines incorporated into 
7 
each set of business assumptions. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What conclusions can you draw from the Gas Planning Depreciation Studies? 
10 
A. 
I believe there are several important conclusions to draw from the analyses provided in the 
11 
depreciation studies filed in the Gas Planning Proceeding.  The first is that significant 
12 
declines in gas throughput and the number of customers in the high electrification business 
13 
assumption scenarios requested by the Commission (i.e., a 50 percent decline in customers 
14 
by 2040 and 90 percent decline by 2050) would result in significant bill increases for those 
15 
customers who remain in 2050 such that there would be serious challenges with 
16 
affordability.  This was true even with more accelerated approaches to depreciation, 
17 
although such approaches did mitigate future rate increases.   
18 
 
 
19 
In contrast to the high electrification scenarios, more moderate electrification scenarios 
20 
such as the Medium Electrification – CEV scenarios, allowed for more stable and 
21 
affordable rates if depreciation were adjusted to properly reflect the decline in throughput 
22 


--- Page 36 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 34 of 48 
 
and the potential loss of gas customers.  Specifically, if the units-of-production method 
1 
were used in a manner aligned with expected declines in gas demand, then shorter-term 
2 
increases in depreciation would mitigate future rate increases by recovering capital more 
3 
rapidly and reducing rate base in a manner aligned with future usage of the gas system.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
How do different gas demand scenarios impact customer rates over the long-term? 
6 
A. 
Figures 1 and 2, below, show the impact of three different gas demand scenarios (these 
7

8 
Studies).  Importantly, the graphs compare the revenue requirement per customer under 
9 
different estimates of future gas throughput, specifically a business-as-usual scenario and 
10 
two scenarios with different levels of declining gas throughput.  The straight line method 
11 
of depreciation is used for the business-as-usual scenario and the units-of-production 
12 
method is used for each of the declining throughput scenarios (i.e., High Electrification 
13 
and Medium Electrification – CEV).5   
14 
 
15 
As shown in the graphs below, if the units-of-production method were used with gas 
16 
throughput levels aligned with the Medium Electrification - CEV, then the total revenue 
17

The analysis, therefore, suggests that there could be a safe, reliable, and affordable path 
5

6

11

Page 37 of 48 
 
there will be affordability challenges even with higher depreciation rates based on the units-
1 
of-production method. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain further the units-of-production method. 
4 
A. 
The units-of-production method is an accepted method recognized by depreciation 
5 
authorities that allocates capital costs equally to production – or consumption – rather than 
6 
in equal amounts to each year.  When this method is used, depreciation accruals may vary 
7 
over an asset’s life based on the consumption that occurs each year.  Thus, for example, if 
8 
throughput were to decline by 50 percent by 2050, depreciation accruals today would be 
9 
twice as high as in 2050, all else equal.  If the decline in consumption is, for example, due 
10 
to a similar decline in customers, this would mean for the units-of-production method each 
11 
generation of customers would pay a similar share of the Companies’ capital costs on a per 
12 
customer basis.  Under such a scenario, if straight-line depreciation were used, then future 
13 
generations would pay a higher share of capital costs on a per customer basis, because the 
14 
accruals in future years would be spread over fewer customers. 
15 
 
16 
The units-of-production method also addresses an issue that focusing only on asset lives 
17 
does not address.  Consider a scenario in which a city street is served by a single gas main.  
18 
Each customer on that street would have their own gas service and meter, but all are served 
19 
from the same gas main.  If, for example, half of the customers electrify their energy usage 
20 
and leave the gas system, the company would retire their services and meters, but the gas 
21 
main would need to remain to provide service to those customers who remain.  Using 
22 


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 38 of 48 
 
straight-line depreciation, these remaining customers would pay a higher proportion of the 
1 
cost of the gas main than those that left the system.  If, however, units-of-production were 
2 
used, then depreciation would be adjusted for the decline in throughput and costs would be 
3 
allocated more equally across the customer base – both those that leave and those who 
4 
remain. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please provide an example to illustrate how the units-of-production method works. 
7 
A. 
To illustrate how the units-of-production method works, consider a simple example of a 
8 
single gas main with a cost of $300,000, a 30-year service life and net salvage of zero.  
9 
Additionally, over the life of the main gas consumption will decline by half, meaning that 
10 
half as much gas will flow through the main in 30 years than is the case today.  Gas 
11 
consumption over the life of the main is illustrated in Figure 3 below. 
12 
Figure 3: Illustration of Gas Throughput Over Life of Main 
13 
 
14 


--- Page 41 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
Page 39 of 48 
Using the straight-line method, the costs of the main would be allocated equally to each of 
1 
the 30 years of service, meaning depreciation would be $10,000 per year.  For the units-of-
2 
production method, costs would be allocated in proportion to the consumption for each 
3 
year.  Accordingly, since consumption declines over the life of the main, depreciation 
4 
would follow a similar pattern.  This is illustrated in Figure 4, below. 
5 
6 
Figure 4: Illustration of Annual Depreciation Expense Based on Straight Line Method 
and Units of Production Method Using Gas Throughput Decline Shown in Figure 3 
7 
8 
When considering only the annual amounts, units-of-production results in higher 
9 
depreciation in the earlier years and lower depreciation in the later years when compared 
10 
to the straight-line method.   However, when comparing accruals to gas consumption a 
11 
different picture emerges.  Figure 5 shows the annual accruals on a per unit-of-consumption 
12 
basis.  Because consumption in 2050 is half the amount in 2050 as in 2020, the straight-
13 
line depreciation is twice as high in 2050 than in 2020 on a per unit basis.  In contrast, the 
14 


--- Page 42 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
Page 40 of 48 
units-of-production method results in equal depreciation amounts each year on a per unit 
1 
basis.  From this standpoint, the units-of-production method provides a fairer and more 
2 
equal allocation of costs than the straight-line method (which results in higher accruals on 
3 
a per unit basis in later years).  If the decline in consumption were similar to the decline in 
4 
the number of customers, then the units-of-production would also result in equal charges 
5 
on a per customer basis over the life of the assets. 
6 
7 
8 
Figure 5:  Illustration of Annual Depreciation Expense On a Per-Unit Basis Using Straight-
Line Method and Units-of-Production Method Using Gas Throughput Decline Shown In 
Figure 3 
9 
10 
Q.
Is the units-of-production method a recognized and accepted method? 
11 
A.
Yes.  First, I note that many authorities recognize that, while the straight-line method is 
12 
widely used, there can be applications in which alternative methods may provide a more 
13 
equitable, systematic and rational allocation of costs.  Indeed, FERC has recognized that 
14 
such situations could arise and for this reason does not explicitly require straight-line 
15 


--- Page 43 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 41 of 48 
 
depreciation in the Uniform System of Accounts.  In FERC Order 618, which was issued 
1 
in Rulemaking RM99-7-000, and modified some portions of the Uniform System of 
2 
Accounts, FERC found: 
3 
We thus initially proposed to adopt for accounting purposes the straight line 
4 
method of depreciation as our standard.  As we noted in the [Notice of 
5 
Proposed Rulemaking], straight line depreciation was the method typically 
6 
used by utilities.  While, in general, we expect that that is likely to continue 
7 
to be the case for most utility property, commenters have persuaded us that 
8 
requiring its universal use would be overly prescriptive.  The primary 
9 
objective of depreciation accounting is to allocate in a systematic and 
10 
rational manner the cost of property to the periods during which the property 
11 
is used in utility operations, i.e., over its estimated useful service life.  As 
12 
Commenters correctly observe, there are methods of depreciation other than 
13 
the straight line method that also meet this objective.6  
14 
The units-of-production method is one such method that is appropriate in some 
15 
circumstances.  This method is recognized by depreciation authorities and has been adopted 
16 
by regulators in some instances.  For example, the definition of service life in the gas 
17 
Uniform System of Accounts includes a reference to a production basis for depreciation: 
18 
Service life means the time between the date gas plant is includible in gas 
19 
plant in service, or gas plant leased to others, and the date of its retirement.  
20 
If depreciation is accounted for on a production basis rather than on a time 
21 
basis, then service life should be measured in terms of the appropriate unit 
22 
of production.7  
23 
The units-of-production method is discussed as an accepted method by the NARUC in 
24 
Public Utility Depreciation Practices.  NARUC explains: 
25 
The unit of production method is similar to age life methods except that in 
26 
place of an estimate of life, total service in terms of units of production is 
27 
 
6 See FERC Order No. 618, 92 FERC ¶ 61,078, p. 10 in Docket No. RM99 7 000 (issued July 27, 2000). 
While this proceeding was related to the electric Uniform System of Accounts, the same concepts would 
apply to gas utilities. 
7 See definition 36 of the gas Uniform System of Accounts. 


--- Page 44 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 42 of 48 
 
estimated.  For example, miles of operation, hours of operation, or unit 
1 
volume of throughput have been used to estimate useful life.  Where plant 
2 
may stand idle for periods of time and then be brought into productive 
3 
operation for varying stretches, the unit of production method may offer a 
4 
more accurate measure of depreciation.  The crucial tests are whether total 
5 
service can be more accurately forecast in production units or in years of 
6 
life span and whether consumption is entirely unrelated to age or is 
7 
reasonably related to age.8  
8 
The units-of-production method has also been used for energy companies.  For example, it 
9 
has been used for natural gas or oil producing facilities for which the production is variable 
10 
over the life of the production facility.  It has also been accepted by the federal Surface 
11 
Transportation Board for certain rail assets.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Does the units-of-production method address many of the challenges that arise from 
14 
the CLCPA related to the recovery of gas assets? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  The potential declines in gas consumption that could result from the CLCPA goals 
16 
present a set of challenges that are much different from those typically presented when 
17 
estimating depreciation.  As the analysis in the Gas Planning Depreciation Studies 
18 
demonstrates, if gas demand declines significantly by 2050, the use of traditional straight-
19 
line depreciation could result in deferring a significant amount of costs to the future, at 
20 
which point there could be far fewer customers to bear these costs.  By matching 
21 
depreciation to throughput on the system, the units-of-production method provides a better 
22 
match of expenses to revenues and mitigates such deferrals to future customers.  However, 
23 
 
8 NARUC, Public Utility Depreciation Practices (1996), p. 52. 


--- Page 45 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 43 of 48 
 
as can be seen in Figures 1 and 2, the future state of the industry also has a significant 
1 
impact.  Even with the units-of-production method, the high electrification scenario results 
2 
in significant bill increases in the future, whereas the Medium Electrification – CEV 
3 
scenario results in more stable bills when the units-of-production method is used. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
How do the current depreciation rates compare to those that result from the units-of-
6 
production method with the Medium Electrification - CEV gas outlook? 
7 
A. 
Figures 6 and 7 below provide a comparison of the depreciation expense as of December 
8 
31, 2021 resulting from the Company’s current depreciation rates to those resulting from 
9 
the units-of-production method with estimated Medium Electrification - CEV gas 
10 
throughput levels.  The units-of-production amounts represent the most equitable 
11 
allocation of cost if gas throughput approximates the Medium Electrification - CEV.  
12 
Similarly, this amount would best match depreciation expense to revenues.  However, as 
13 
can be seen in Figures 6 and 7, the Companies’ current depreciation rates produce much 
14 
lower depreciation than the units-of-production method. 
15

Page 45 of 48 
 
As the charts show, the Companies’ current depreciation rates are significantly lower than 
1 
needed in the context of the CLCPA.  For both KEDNY and KEDLI, the units-of-
2 
production depreciation rates are nearly double the Companies’ current depreciation rates.  
3 
Keep in mind that the units-of-production amounts shown above are based on the 
4 
Companies’ Medium Electrification - CEV, which incorporates a more moderate degree of 
5 
electrification to meet the CLCPA goals.  For a higher electrification scenario, such as set 
6 
forth in the CAC’s Final Scoping Plan, the units-of-production depreciation would be even 
7 
higher.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Why are the Companies’ current depreciation rates so much lower than the units-of-
10 
production method? 
11 
A. 
There are two primary reasons.  The first is that, as discussed above, the CLCPA 
12 
necessitates higher depreciation than was the case in the past.  However, the Companies’ 
13 
depreciation rates are too low even for business-as-usual circumstances.  As discussed 
14 
previously, this is primarily the result of too little net salvage being included in the current 
15 
depreciation rates.  The impact is illustrated in Figures 6 and 7.  The second bar in each the 
16 
graphs show the annual depreciation expense resulting from the Depreciation Studies.  
17 
Reflecting more realistic levels of net salvage results in higher depreciation expense, and 
18 
the studies recommend increases of 29 percent for KEDLI and 50 percent for KEDNY.  
19 
However, the inadequate levels of net salvage in current depreciation rates has also 
20 
contributed to reserve deficiencies.  The third bars show the total depreciation expense as 
21 
of December 31, 2021 including the amortization of a portion of the reserve deficiencies 
22 


--- Page 48 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 46 of 48 
 
consistent with the Companies’ proposal in this case.  For each company, the resultant 
1 
increases are more than 50 percent of current depreciation 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Have the Companies proposed to use the units-of-production for this rate case? 
4 
A. 
No.  As shown in Figures 6 and 7 above, not only is there a need to increase depreciation 
5 
due to changes to the gas system resulting from the CLCPA, but the Companies’ 
6 
depreciation expense is too low for even a business-as-usual scenario.  The Companies’ 
7 
proposal is to begin to phase in the needed increases in depreciation, first by moving 
8 
towards the results of the Depreciation Studies using the straight-line method.  Once 
9 
depreciation has effectively caught up to where it should be under business-as-usual 
10 
circumstances, it can then move further towards where it should be in the context of the 
11 
CLCPA.  The Companies recognize that there is a need to balance the impacts on future 
12 
customers with those on current customers and this phased-in approach is consistent with 
13 
finding the proper balance. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
The Companies’ proposal is to begin to move depreciation towards the higher levels 
16 
needed.  Are there risks of deferring increases in depreciation? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  In the context of the CLCPA, there are multiple risks involved with failing to 
18 
incorporate the potential impact of these goals on depreciation.  The first is the potential 
19 
for future customers to have to bear the impact of cost recovery associated with 
20 
infrastructure that is not used to meet their energy needs.  That is, assets could be retired 
21 
prior to the full costs being recovered through depreciation, raising the risk of 
22 


--- Page 49 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 47 of 48 
 
intergenerational inequity.  If depreciation rates are too low today, future customers will 
1 
have to pay too high of a share of the cost of the Companies’ assets as a transition to other 
2 
energy sources takes place.  Second, there is the risk that customers will leave the system, 
3 
which would push additional costs to the future customers that remain.  Finally, these risks 
4 
are related – if depreciation is higher in the future and customers have left the system, there 
5 
will be fewer customers to pay the remaining costs of the Companies’ assets, further 
6 
compounding intergenerational inequity resulting from depreciation rates being too low 
7 
today. 
8 
 
9 
It is also important to recognize that the risks are asymmetric.  If it turns out that 
10 
depreciation is too high today then depreciation rates will be adjusted through the 
11 
amortizations of reserve deficiencies or, preferably, the use of the remaining life technique.  
12 
As a result, customers will pay lower costs for depreciation in the future.  Additionally, 
13 
rate base will be lower than it otherwise would have been, further reducing costs for 
14 
customers.  However, as discussed above, if depreciation rates are too low today – and if 
15 
customers electrify and leave the gas system – then the impact on future customers will be 
16 
much greater because there will likely be fewer customers to pay the remaining capital 
17 
costs.  They will also have to pay a higher return on rate base, further compounding the 
18 
issue.  Lastly, they could have to bear the costs of assets that retired without being fully 
19 
recovered, which is also inequitable. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 50 ---
Testimony of Ned W. Allis 
 
Page 48 of 48 
 
Given all of these considerations, in my judgment the cost implications of the Companies’ 
1 
proposal are at the lower end of the range of reasonableness for depreciation in this case.  
2 
Depreciation will likely need to continue to increase in future depreciation studies.  As a 
3 
result, it would not be equitable or appropriate to materially reduce the Companies’ 
4 
depreciation proposal in this case. 
5 
 
6 
Q.  
Does this conclude your testimony? 
7 
A. 
Yes. 
8

Testimony of Ned W. Allis

THE BROOKLYN UNION GAS COMPANY (“KEDNY”) 
DEPRECIATION STUDY 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
Pursuant to The Brooklyn Union Gas Company (“KEDNY” or “Company”) request, 
Gannett Fleming Valuation and Rate Consultants, LLC (“Gannett Fleming”) conducted a 
depreciation study related to KEDNY’s gas plant as of December 31, 2021. The purpose 
of this study was to determine the annual depreciation accrual rates and amounts for book 
and ratemaking purposes. 
The depreciation rates are based on the straight-line method using the average 
service life (“ASL”) procedure and were applied on a whole life basis. The calculations 
were based on attained ages and estimated average service life and net salvage for each 
depreciable group of assets. 
In general, depreciation expense has trended higher for the Company’s gas plant 
in service.  One of the primary drivers of the increase in depreciation is more negative net 
salvage estimates for several accounts.  These changes are the result of both trends in 
the data for more negative net salvage as well as that the Company’s current depreciation 
rates do not include a sufficient level of net salvage.  One consideration related to service 
life estimates for gas utilities in New York is the impacts of greenhouse gas emissions 
reductions on gas assets, which could impact both the service lives of gas assets and the 
most appropriate depreciation method.   The Company’s current average service lives for 
many accounts are at the upper end of the range of service lives seen throughout the 
industry for these types of assets.  While this study does not recommend specific 
reductions in service lives resulting New York’s greenhouse gas emissions legislation, 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company ("KEDNY") 
December 31, 2021
LLL
Exhibit ___(NWA-1) 
Page 6 of 260


--- Page 59 ---
these considerations do provide reasons to, at a minimum, limit any increases in service 
lives for many types of gas assets.     
Gannett Fleming recommends the calculated annual depreciation accrual rates set 
forth herein apply specifically to gas plant in service as of December 31, 2021 as 
summarized by Table 1 of the study. Supporting analysis and calculations are provided 
within the study.  For the accounts included in the study, the study results set forth an 
annual depreciation expense of $215,318,342 for gas plant when applied to depreciable 
plant balances for accounts studied as of December 31, 2021. Additionally, the study 
estimates a negative reserve imbalance (also referred to as “reserve deficiencies”) of 
$397,968,068 for gas plant.

THE BROOKLYN UNION GAS COMPANY (“KEDNY”) 
DEPRECIATION STUDY 
 
PART I.  INTRODUCTION 
SCOPE 
 
 
This report sets forth the results of the depreciation study for The Brooklyn Union 
Gas Company (“KEDNY” or “Company”), to determine the annual depreciation accrual 
rates and amounts for book purposes applicable to the original cost of gas plant as of 
December 31, 2021. KEDNY is a utility company located in Brooklyn, NY and provides 
gas service to more than one million customers in the boroughs of Brooklyn, Staten Island 
and a portion of Queens.  The depreciation rates and amounts recommended in this study 
are based on the straight line whole life method of depreciation. This report also describes 
the concepts, methods and judgments which underlie the recommended annual 
depreciation accrual rates related to gas plant in service as of December 31, 2021. 
 
The service life and net salvage estimates resulting from the study were based on 
informed judgment which incorporated analyses of historical plant retirement data as 
recorded through 2021, a review of Company practice and outlook as they relate to plant 
operation and retirement, and consideration of current practices in the industry, including 
knowledge of service lives and net salvage estimates used for other utility companies. 
PLAN OF REPORT 
 
Part I, Introduction, contains statements with respect to the plan of the report, and 
the basis of the study. Part II, Estimation of Survivor Curves, presents descriptions of the 
considerations and the methods used in the service life and net salvage studies. Part III, 
Service Life Considerations, presents the factors and judgment utilized in the average 
service life analysis.  Part IV, Net Salvage Considerations, presents the judgment utilized 
for the net salvage study. Part V, Calculation of Annual and Accrued Depreciation, 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company ("KEDNY") 
December 31, 2021

describes the procedures used in the calculation of group depreciation. Part VI, Results 
of Study, presents summaries by depreciable group of annual depreciation accrual rates 
and amounts, as well as the calculated accrued depreciation, reserve variations and 
composite remaining lives. Part VII, Service Life Statistics presents the statistical analysis 
of service life estimates. Part VIII, Net Salvage Statistics sets forth the statistical 
indications of net salvage percents, and Part IX, Detailed Depreciation Calculations 
presents the detailed tabulations of annual and accrued depreciation.  
BASIS OF THE STUDY 
Depreciation 
 
Depreciation, in public utility regulation, is the loss in service value not restored by 
current maintenance, incurred in connection with the consumption or prospective 
retirement of utility plant in the course of service from causes which are known to be in 
current operation and against which the utility is not protected by insurance.  Among 
causes to be given consideration are wear and tear, deterioration, action of the elements, 
inadequacy, obsolescence, changes in the art, changes in demand, and the requirements 
of public authorities. 
 
Depreciation, as used in accounting, is a method of distributing fixed capital costs, 
less net salvage, over a period of time by allocating annual amounts to expense.  Each 
annual amount of such depreciation expense is part of that year's total cost of providing 
utility service.  Normally, the period of time over which the fixed capital cost is allocated 
to the cost of service is equal to the period of time over which an item renders service, 
that is, the item's service life.  The most prevalent method of allocation is to distribute an 
equal amount of cost to each year of service life.  This method is known as the straight-
line method of depreciation. 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company ("KEDNY") 
December 31, 2021
I-3
Exhibit ___(NWA-1) 
Page 10 of 260


--- Page 63 ---
 
 
The annual depreciation was calculated by the straight-line method using the 
average service life procedure and the whole life basis. The calculations were based on 
original cost, attained ages, and estimates of service lives and net salvage. The straight-
line method, average service life procedure is a commonly used depreciation calculation 
procedure that has been widely accepted in jurisdictions throughout North America. 
Gannett Fleming recommends its use in this study. While the remaining life basis is more 
commonly used in jurisdictions in North America, the whole life basis has traditionally 
been used in New York.  The whole life basis is recommended for this study. 
Service Life and Net Salvage Estimates 
 
The service life and net salvage estimates used in the depreciation calculations 
were based on informed judgment which incorporated a review of management’s plans, 
policies and outlook, a general knowledge of the electric and gas utility industries, and 
comparisons of the service life and net salvage estimates from our studies of other 
utilities. The use of survivor curves to reflect the expected dispersion of retirement 
provides a consistent method of estimating depreciation for utility plant.  Iowa-type 
survivor curves were used to depict the estimated survivor curves for the plant accounts 
not subject to amortization accounting. 
 
The procedure for estimating service lives consisted of compiling historical data 
for the plant accounts or depreciable groups, analyzing this history through the use of 
widely accepted techniques, and forecasting the survivor characteristics for each 
depreciable group on the basis of interpretations of the historical data analyses and the 
probable future. The combination of the historical experience and the estimated future 
yielded estimated survivor curves from which the average service lives were derived.

PART II.  ESTIMATION OF SURVIVOR CURVES 
 
The calculation of annual depreciation based on the straight line method requires 
the estimation of survivor curves and the selection of group depreciation procedures.  The 
estimation of survivor curves is discussed below and the development of net salvage is 
discussed in later sections of this report. 
SURVIVOR CURVES 
 
The use of an average service life for a property group implies that the various 
units in the group have different lives.  Thus, the average life may be obtained by 
determining the separate lives of each of the units or by constructing a survivor curve by 
plotting the number of units which survive at successive ages. 
 
The survivor curve graphically depicts the amount of property existing at each age 
throughout the life of an original group.  From the survivor curve, the average life of the 
group, the remaining life expectancy, the probable life, and the frequency curve can be 
calculated.  In Figure 1, a typical smooth survivor curve and the derived curves are 
illustrated.  The average life is obtained by calculating the area under the survivor curve, 
from age zero to the maximum age, and dividing this area by the ordinate at age zero.  
The remaining life expectancy at any age can be calculated by obtaining the area under 
the curve, from the observation age to the maximum age, and dividing this area by the 
percent surviving at the observation age.  For example, in Figure 1, the remaining life at 
age 30 is equal to the crosshatched area under the survivor curve divided by 29.5 percent 
surviving at age 30.  The probable life at any age is developed by adding the age and 
remaining life.  If the probable life of the property is calculated for each year of age, the 
probable life curve shown in the chart can be developed.  The frequency curve presents 
the number of units retired in each age interval. It is derived by obtaining the differences 
between the amount of property surviving at the beginning and at the end of each interval. 
_____________________________________________________________________________________________
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company ("KEDNY") 
December 31, 2021
II-2

--- Page 66 ---
 
 
This study has incorporated the use of Iowa curves developed from a retirement 
rate analysis of historical retirement history.  A discussion of the concepts of survivor 
curves and of the development of survivor curves using the retirement rate method is 
presented below. 
Iowa Type Curves 
 
The range of survivor characteristics usually experienced by utility and industrial 
properties is encompassed by a system of generalized survivor curves known as the Iowa 
type curves.  There are four families in the Iowa system, labeled in accordance with the 
location of the modes of the retirements (or the portion of the frequency curve with the 
highest level of retirements) in relationship to the average life and the relative height of 
the modes.  The left moded curves, presented in Figure 2, are those in which the greatest 
frequency of retirement occurs to the left of, or prior to, average service life.  The 
symmetrical moded curves, presented in Figure 3, are those in which the greatest 
frequency of retirement occurs at average service life.  The right moded curves, presented 
in Figure 4, are those in which the greatest frequency occurs to the right of, or after, 
average service life.  The origin moded curves, presented in Figure 5, are those in which 
the greatest frequency of retirement occurs at the origin, or immediately after age zero.  
The letter designation of each family of curves (L, S, R or O) represents the location of 
the mode of the associated frequency curve with respect to the average service life.  The 
numbers represent the relative heights of the modes of the frequency curves within each 
family.  A higher number designates a higher mode curve. 
 
The Iowa curves were developed at the Iowa State College Engineering 
Experiment Station through an extensive process of observation and classification of the 
ages at which industrial property had been retired.  A report of the study which resulted 
in the classification of property survivor characteristics into 18 type curves, which 
constitute three of the four families, was published in 1935 in the form of the Experiment 
Station’s Bulletin 125.

These curve types have also been presented in subsequent Experiment Station 
bulletins and in the text, "Engineering Valuation and Depreciation."1  In 1957, Frank V. B. 
Couch, Jr., an Iowa State College graduate student, submitted a thesis presenting his 
development of the fourth family consisting of the four O type survivor curves. 
Retirement Rate Method of Analysis 
 
The retirement rate method is an actuarial method of deriving survivor curves using 
the average rates at which property of each age group is retired.  The method relates to 
property groups for which aged accounting experience is available and is the method 
used to develop the original stub survivor curves in this study.  The method (also known 
as the annual rate method) is illustrated through the use of an example in the following 
text and is also explained in several publications including "Statistical Analyses of 
Industrial Property Retirements,"2 "Engineering Valuation and Depreciation,"3 and 
"Depreciation Systems."4 
   
The average rate of retirement used in the calculation of the percent surviving for 
the survivor curve (life table) requires two sets of data:  first, the property retired during a 
period of observation, identified by the property's age at retirement; and second, the 
property exposed to retirement at the beginning of the age intervals during the same 
period.  The period of observation is referred to as the experience band.  The band of 
years which represent the installation dates of the property exposed to retirement during 
the experience band is referred to as the placement band.  An example of the calculations 
used in the development of a life table follows.  The example includes schedules of annual 
aged property transactions, a schedule of plant exposed to retirement, a life table and 
illustrations of smoothing the stub survivor curve.  
 
1Marston, Anson, Robley Winfrey and Jean C. Hempstead.  Engineering Valuation and Depreciation, 
2nd Edition.  New York,  McGraw-Hill Book Company.  1953. 
 2Winfrey, Robley, Statistical Analyses of Industrial Property Retirements.  Iowa State College, 
Engineering Experiment Station, Bulletin 125.  1935.
